=== PROMPT VARIANT ===

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
For integers l, r, let [l, r] denote the set of all integers from l through r. That is, [l, r] = \lbrace l, l+1, l+2, \ldots, r-1, r\rbrace.
You are given N pairs of integers (L_1, R_1), (L_2, R_2), \ldots, (L_N, R_N).
Based on these pairs, consider an undirected graph G defined as follows:

- It has N vertices numbered 1, 2, \ldots, N.
- For all i, j \in [1, N], there is an undirected edge between vertices i and j if and only if the intersection of [L_i, R_i] and [L_j, R_j] is empty.

In addition, for each i = 1, 2, \ldots, N, define the weight of vertex i to be W_i.
You are given Q queries about G. Process these queries in the order they are given.
For each i = 1, 2, \ldots, Q, the i-th query is the following:

You are given integers s_i and t_i (both between 1 and N, inclusive) such that s_i \neq t_i. Determine whether there exists a path from vertex s_i to vertex t_i in G. If it exists, print the minimum possible weight of such a path.

Here, the weight of a path from vertex s to vertex t is defined as the sum of the weights of the vertices on that path (including both endpoints s and t).

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
W_1 W_2 \cdots W_N
L_1 R_1
L_2 R_2
\vdots
L_N R_N
Q
s_1 t_1
s_2 t_2
\vdots
s_Q t_Q

Output

Print Q lines.
For each i = 1, 2, \ldots, Q, on the i-th line, if there exists a path from vertex s_i to vertex t_i, print the minimum possible weight of such a path, and print -1 otherwise.

Constraints


- 2 \leq N \leq 2 \times 10^5
- 1 \leq Q \leq 2 \times 10^5
- 1 \leq W_i \leq 10^9
- 1 \leq L_i \leq R_i \leq 2N
- 1 \leq s_i, t_i \leq N
- s_i \neq t_i
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

5
5 1 4 2 2
2 4
1 2
7 8
4 5
2 7
3
1 4
4 3
5 2

Sample Output 1

11
6
-1

G is a graph with four undirected edges: \lbrace 1, 3\rbrace, \lbrace 2, 3\rbrace, \lbrace 2, 4\rbrace, \lbrace 3, 4\rbrace.

- For the first query, there is a path from vertex 1 to vertex 4 given by 1 \to 3 \to 4. The weight of this path is W_1 + W_3 + W_4 = 5 + 4 + 2 = 11, and this is the minimum possible.
- For the second query, there is a path from vertex 4 to vertex 3 given by 4 \to 3. The weight of this path is W_4 + W_3 = 2 + 4 = 6, and this is the minimum possible.
- For the third query, there is no path from vertex 5 to vertex 2. Hence, print -1.

Sample Input 2

8
44 75 49 4 78 79 12 32
5 13
10 16
6 8
6 15
12 15
5 7
1 15
1 2
5
5 6
3 2
7 5
4 5
5 4

Sample Output 2

157
124
-1
114
114

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

In interval order comparability graph, distance between a and b can be characterized by existence of alternating sequences of intervals "inside" a∩b? Let's derive.

Given two intervals a=[L_a,R_a], b=[L_b,R_b]. If they are disjoint, distance 1.

If they overlap. For a path a=v0, v1, ..., vk=b. Consecutive intervals disjoint. Consider the first step from a to v1. Either v1 is left of a (R_v1 < L_a) or right of a (L_v1 > R_a). Similarly last step to b: v_{k-1} left of b or right of b. The path can alternate.

Maybe we can view each edge as crossing a "gap" between intervals. If v1 left of a, then v1's R < L_a. Since a and b overlap, v1 may or may not be left of b. If v1 left of b, then v1-b edge, giving path a-v1-b length 2. If not, v1 intersects b (because not left; and since R_v1 < L_a <= R_b, it may be inside/left part of b). Similarly if v1 right of a, etc.

Thus a path of length >2 occurs when intermediate intervals are "inside" the overlap/one endpoint but outside the other.

Let's define for each vertex v, its interval [L,R]. Edge from u to v if R_u < L_v (u left of v) or R_v < L_u (v left of u). Suppose we orient edges from left interval to right interval. An undirected path can be seen as sequence where each step either increases coordinate (left-to-right) or decreases (right-to-left). In terms of interval order, each edge is a comparability; orient from smaller (left) to larger (right). Then an undirected path corresponds to a sequence in poset where adjacent elements are comparable, but orientation can alternate. In a poset, any undirected path in comparability graph can be "compressed" to a zigzag: a1 < a2 > a3 < a4 ...? Because if two consecutive comparabilities have same orientation (a < b < c), then a < c, so edge a-c and can skip b. Thus in a shortest path, orientations must alternate. So path is a zigzag in interval order: v0 < v1 > v2 < v3 > ... or v0 > v1 < v2 > ... (where < means interval left of). This is key.

In interval order, x < y iff R_x < L_y. So a zigzag path alternates between intervals to left and right.

In an alternating path, if we orient from left to right, an edge can be traversed either up (from left interval to right interval) or down (from right to left). But a shortest path alternates up/down. So we can model each step as moving from a vertex to any vertex on the opposite side. State maybe "current vertex is left of next" (we are going up) or "current vertex is right of next" (going down). But because next step must be opposite, from a vertex v, if previous edge was up (we arrived at v from a left interval, so v is right of previous), then next edge must be down: leave v to an interval left of v. If previous edge was down (arrived from right interval, v is left), next must be up: leave to interval right of v. Thus at each vertex, the allowed outgoing direction depends on previous direction. This is like a graph with directed edges representing "go left" or "go right" alternately. We can create two copies: state 0 = need to go right? Let's define.

At vertex v, if we are at v and the next edge will be to a right interval (v left of next), call state R (up). If next edge will be to a left interval (next left of v), call state L (down). But after taking an edge to neighbor w, the next required direction is opposite:
- From v state R: choose w with L_w > R_v (v left of w). Edge v-w. At w, since we came from left, next must go left: state L.
- From v state L: choose w with R_w < L_v (w left of v). At w, next must go right: state R.
So transitions alternate states. Start at s can choose either R or L (first edge direction). End at t can be reached from either direction; no need to continue. This is exactly a directed graph on 2N states with transitions:
R(v) -> L(w) if L_w > R_v (w right of v)
L(v) -> R(w) if R_w < L_v (w left of v)

A path of length 3 from s to t can have two orientation patterns:
Pattern A: s > x < y > t. Here:
- x ∈ Left(s) (R_x < L_s)
- y ∈ Right(x) (L_y > R_x) and y ∈ Left? Wait y > t means t ∈ Left(y): R_t < L_y. Also x < y means R_x < L_y. So y is to the right of both x and t. Thus y ∈ Right(x) and y ∈ Right(t)? Pattern B: s < x > y < t. Symmetric: x ∈ Right(s), y ∈ Left(t), and y < x (R_y < L_x). Weight W_x+W_y.

So distance 3 min is min of:
A: min_{x: R_x < L_s} min_{y: L_y > max(R_t, R_x)} (W_x + W_y)
B: min_{x: L_x > R_s} min_{y: R_y < min(L_t, L_x)} (W_x + W_y)

### Connectivity of G

Need compute connected components of graph where edge if disjoint. Since diameter ≤3 if connected, but components can be found perhaps by union of intervals that are connected via paths length up to? We can build graph G implicitly. Need union intervals that are disjoint. Since edge if R_i < L_j or R_j < L_i. But both adjacent to C, so C connects them (path A-C-B length2) even if A,B overlap. Thus for connectivity, from an interval i, it is enough to union i with the "leftmost" or "rightmost" neighbor? If i has any left neighbor, i is connected to all left neighbors via? Not directly, but i connected to each left neighbor. To propagate connectivity among all vertices, we can union i with some representative left neighbor and representative right neighbor? Suppose i has left neighbors set Lset. If we union i with one left neighbor a, then i and a connected. But i is connected to another left neighbor b via i-b edge, so b should be in same component as i. If we don't union i-b directly, maybe b connected to a through i? Actually i-b edge connects b to i, and i-a connects i to a, so b connected to a via i. So if we union i with a only, b is not unioned with i unless we process b similarly. But b may not have i as neighbor? b is left of i, so i is right neighbor of b. If when processing b, we union b with some right neighbor representative, we could choose i? Need ensure.

Maybe we can union each interval with:
- The interval with minimum R among those with R < L_i? (left neighbor with smallest R? Actually any left neighbor)
- The interval with maximum L among those with L > R_i? (right neighbor)

Consider a vertex v. All left neighbors are intervals ending before L_v. If we union v with the left neighbor having minimum L? Let's define a representative. Since all left neighbors are adjacent to v, they are all in same component as v. If we union v with one left neighbor, that neighbor's component includes v. But other left neighbors need to be connected to v's component. They may be connected via v if we union them too, but we don't. However, if we process those other left neighbors, they may union with v as their right representative? Not guaranteed if v is not the "max L" among right neighbors. We can choose for each vertex a right representative with maximum L? Let's design sweep.

Alternative: Use interval graph complement components. There is known algorithm: Sort intervals by L. Maintain the interval with maximum R among processed? Let's derive.

In G, edge if disjoint. If we sort by L ascending. For current interval i, all previous intervals with R < L_i are left neighbors. Among previous intervals, if the maximum R so far is < L_i, then all previous intervals? They may overlap if R >= L_i.

For connectivity, if i has at least one left neighbor, then i is connected to the component containing that neighbor. Which left neighbor to choose? The one with maximum R among R < L_i? Alternative connectivity algorithm: Since diameter ≤3, we can compute components by BFS/DFS with range queries? For each unvisited vertex, explore its neighbors using data structures to find all unvisited intervals with R < L_v or L > R_v, and remove them. This is standard for implicit graphs with range edges. We can run BFS over G: maintain set of unvisited vertices. To visit neighbors of u, we need find all unvisited vertices with R < L_u and all with L > R_u. We can support this with segment trees / sorted sets and delete visited vertices. Total O(N log N). This directly computes connected components! Since graph has N vertices and edges implicit, BFS can enumerate each vertex once by range reporting. Need be careful: When visiting u, we need iterate over all unvisited neighbors. We can use two data structures keyed by R and L:
- For left neighbors: vertices with R < L_u. We need report all unvisited vertices in this set, add to queue, remove them.
- For right neighbors: vertices with L > R_u. Report all unvisited vertices in this set, add, remove.

If we remove each vertex when discovered, each vertex reported once. Data structures: balanced BST of unvisited vertices sorted by R for left query, and sorted by L for right query. In Python, need implement efficient O(log N) deletion and range extraction. Could use heap? Since queries are dynamic (delete arbitrary). We can use segment tree storing min/max? Let's design.

We have N up to 2e5. We can maintain two sorted lists with DSU "next unvisited" maybe:
- Sort vertices by R. For a query R < X, we need repeatedly find an unvisited vertex with smallest R? Actually all with R < X. We can use a min-heap of (R, vertex) for unvisited? But after deleting vertices, heap lazy. To report all with R < X, pop from min-heap while R < X; if vertex unvisited, visit. This works because we only need any unvisited with R < X; min-heap gives smallest R. But what about vertices with larger R but still <X? Popping min will eventually pop all with R < X if we continue until min R >= X. Since heap contains all unvisited vertices (lazy deletion), we can pop all unvisited with R < X. That reports all left neighbors. Complexity: Each vertex popped once from heap when visited; but there may be stale entries for visited vertices popped later. Total heap operations O(N log N). Good.

- For right neighbors: need vertices with L > R_u. We can use max-heap of (-L, vertex) or sorted by L descending. To report all unvisited with L > X, pop from max-heap while L > X. This reports all right neighbors. Again each vertex popped once.

But careful: If we use two separate heaps, a vertex may be visited and removed from unvisited set, but stale entries remain in heaps. When popping stale, skip. Need also ensure not push duplicates? We push each vertex once into each heap initially. When a vertex is visited, it remains in other heap as stale; later popped and skipped. Good.

BFS:
visited = [False]*N
heaps:
heapR = min-heap of (R_i, i) for all i.
heapL = max-heap of (-L_i, i) for all i (or sort descending).
For each vertex start 0..N-1:
 if not visited:
   comp_id++; BFS(start)
BFS:
 visited[start]=True; queue=[start]
 while queue:
   u = pop
   # left neighbors: R < L_u
   while heapR and heapR[0][0] < L_u:
      r,i = heappop(heapR)
      if not visited[i]:
         visited[i]=True; comp[i]=cid; queue.append(i)
      # if visited, skip; but note if visited, it was already removed from unvisited, but stale. We still popped it from heapR. That's okay; it's gone from heapR. But what if a vertex was visited via heapL and not yet popped from heapR; popping it now as stale removes it from heapR. Good.
   # right neighbors: L > R_u
   while heapL and -heapL[0][0] > R_u:
      l,i = heappop(heapL) (store -L)
      if not visited[i]:
         visited[i]=True; comp[i]=cid; queue.append(i)
      # skip
This BFS visits all neighbors of u. But is it sufficient to discover all vertices in component? In BFS, when we process u, we need to enqueue all unvisited neighbors. The heap method with min-heap by R: It pops all unvisited vertices with R < L_u. However, if some unvisited vertex with R < L_u has been popped earlier as stale? It can't be unvisited if popped earlier; if it was visited, skip. If it was not visited, it remains in heap. Good. But the while condition uses heapR[0][0] < L_u. Suppose the smallest R in heap is for a visited vertex with R < L_u; we pop and skip. Continue until smallest unvisited R >= L_u or heap empty. This indeed reports all unvisited vertices with R < L_u because heap contains all unvisited vertices (plus stale), and min-heap order ensures all with R < threshold are at front. Similarly max-heap for L > R_u.

This BFS computes components in O(N log N). Great! No need prove p/q.

Let's formalize distance 3 patterns for overlapping s,t. Since s,t overlap, no direct. A 3-edge path must alternate. There are two patterns:

- y ∈ Right(t): L_y > R_t.
- x < y: R_x < L_y.

- x ∈ Right(s): L_x > R_s.
- y ∈ Left(t): R_y < L_t.
- y < x: R_y < L_x.

### Proof of diameter ≤3 for connected components of G

We can present in final? Only code needed, but for confidence.

Let G be disjointness graph of intervals. Suppose there is a shortest path v0, v1, ..., vk with k≥4. Since it's shortest, no shortcuts. Orient each edge by interval order; consecutive orientations alternate (otherwise if vi-1 < vi < vi+1 then vi-1 < vi+1, edge, shortcut; similarly for >). WLOG v0 > v1 (v1 left of v0). Then v1 < v2, v2 > v3, v3 < v4, ... (for k≥4, v3 < v4). Consider v1 and v4. If they are comparable, then v0-v1-v4 (or v0-v1-v4? If v1<v4, edge v1-v4, path v0-v1-v4 length2; if v4<v1, edge v1-v4, path v0-v1-v4 length2) gives a path from v0 to v4 of length 2, contradicting that the subpath v0-v1-v2-v3-v4 is shortest (length4) between v0 and v4. Thus v1 and v4 are incomparable (overlap). Similarly v0 and v3 are incomparable (otherwise path v0-v3-v4 length2? If v0<v3 or v3<v0, edge v0-v3, then v0-v3-v4 length2; yes).
Now from v1 < v0: R1 < L0.
From v0 and v3 overlap: L0 <= R3 (and L3 <= R0). Thus R1 < L0 <= R3, so R1 < R3.
From v3 < v4: R3 < L4.
From v1 and v4 overlap: L4 <= R1 (and L1 <= R4). Thus R3 < L4 <= R1, so R3 < R1.
Contradiction. Therefore no shortest path length ≥4. Hence diameter ≤3. Nice. This proof assumes v0 > v1; if v0 < v1, mirror (or reverse order) gives contradiction. Good.

Need also ensure if k>4, subpath v0..v4 is shortest between v0 and v4. In a shortest path, any subpath is shortest; if there were shorter v0-v4, replacing would shorten whole path. So contradiction.

Thus connected components have diameter ≤3. Our BFS components correct.

Now, distance formulas for overlapping s,t.

If s,t overlap, no direct edge. A path of length 2: s-x-t. For x adjacent to both. Since s,t overlap, x cannot be left of one and right of the other (prove: if R_x < L_s and L_x > R_t, then R_x < L_s <= R_t < L_x, contradict L_x <= R_x; similarly). So x is left of both or right of both. Thus common neighbor min = prefR[min(L_s,L_t)] +? Actually min weight among left of both: R_x < L_s and R_x < L_t -> R_x < min(L_s,L_t). Right of both: L_x > R_s and L_x > R_t -> L_x > max(R_s,R_t). So dist2 formula.

A path of length 3: s-x-y-t. Since shortest, orientations alternate. If first edge s > x (x left of s), then x < y and y > t. Conditions: R_x < L_s, R_x < L_y, R_t < L_y. Because s,t overlap, R_x < L_s <= R_t < L_y, so R_x < L_y automatically. Thus any x left of s and y right of t works. Min extra = prefR[L_s] + suffL[R_t]. If first edge s < x, similarly extra = suffL[R_s] + prefR[L_t]. Note if x or y also adjacent to t/s, that would give length2 path, but length3 formula still valid; min may choose it but dist2 would be smaller. Since we take min, okay. Need ensure if x and y are same? We argued impossible because s,t overlap and x left of s, y right of t cannot be same. For Pattern B similarly.

What if s,t overlap and a length 3 path has orientations not alternating? A shortest path won't, but our formula for min path weight should consider only shortest paths. Since any non-alternating path can be shortcut to shorter path (same or lower weight because positive weights? Shortcut removes vertices, so lower weight). Thus minimum weight path is a shortest (minimum edge count among min weight? Actually if a longer path has lower weight than a shorter path? Since weights positive, any path with more vertices has more positive weights than a subpath? If you shortcut by removing vertices, the resulting path weight is strictly less. So a minimum weight path will also be a shortest path in terms of edge count? Not necessarily: a path with 2 edges weight 100, path with 3 edges weight 10? But if there is a 2-edge path, can a 3-edge path have lower weight? Yes, as noted: common neighbor heavy, two light vertices. But the 3-edge path is not a shortcut of the 2-edge path; they are different. However, any path with same orientation non-alternating can be shortcut to fewer edges with lower weight, so a minimum weight path will be alternating (no consecutive same orientation). Thus length can be 1,2,3. We consider all lengths 1,2,3. Good.

Need consider if there is a path of length 2 and a path of length 3 with lower weight; our min handles.

Now, is dist3 formula maybe overcounts because it chooses x and y independently, but if x and y are not adjacent? We proved automatic due to overlap. Let's double-check with an example where s,t overlap but prefR[L_s] and suffL[R_t] finite. Let s=[5,10], t=[1,6]. x left of s: R_x<5. y right of t: L_y>6. Then R_x<5<=6<L_y, so R_x<L_y, so x<y edge. Yes.

Pattern B: x right of s: L_x>10. y left of t: R_y<1. Since L_x>10>=? s,t overlap R_s=10 >= L_t=1 > R_y, so R_y<L_x, edge. Yes.

Thus formulas solid.

Now, need precompute prefR and suffL with weights. But wait, prefR[L_s] is min W_i with R_i < L_s. This includes s itself? R_s < L_s false because R_s>=L_s. So no. It may include t? If t left of s? But s,t overlap, t not left of s (R_t < L_s false because overlap implies R_t >= L_s). So no. Good.

suffL[R_t] includes t? L_t > R_t false. No.

Now, if s,t overlap and prefR[L_s] + suffL[R_t] finite, the path s-x-y-t uses x left of s and y right of t. Are we sure x and y are distinct and x-y edge? yes. But what if x is also right of t? impossible; y left of s? impossible. Good.

Now, connectivity BFS with heaps: Need ensure it computes components of G exactly. Let's prove BFS range reporting:
We maintain heapR of all vertices keyed by R, with lazy deletion. When processing u, while heapR min R < L_u, pop. If popped vertex unvisited, it is a left neighbor of u (R < L_u), so edge u-v; we visit it. If popped vertex already visited, skip. Since heapR contains all unvisited vertices plus stale visited, and min-heap order, the loop will pop exactly all unvisited vertices with R < L_u (and some stale). Thus all unvisited left neighbors are discovered. Similarly heapL with max L. Starting from each unvisited vertex, BFS explores all edges to unvisited vertices; standard BFS visits entire component. Complexity O(N log N) because each vertex is pushed once into each heap initially, and popped at most once from each heap (when encountered as stale or fresh). Wait if a vertex is visited via heapL, it remains in heapR; later when heapR pops it as stale, it is removed. So each vertex popped once from heapR and once from heapL. Good.

Implementation plan:

```
import sys, heapq

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    it = iter(data)
    N = next(it)
    W = [0]*N
    for i in range(N): W[i] = next(it)
    L = [0]*N
    R = [0]*N
    for i in range(N):
        L[i] = next(it); R[i] = next(it)
    Q = next(it)
    queries = [(next(it)-1, next(it)-1) for _ in range(Q)]  # maybe process after precompute; but need Q after intervals. We can store.
```
Need be careful with iterator and Q. Since we need build heaps before queries? We can process queries after precompute; store queries list. Q up to 2e5, okay.

But data parsing with iter and next: If we store queries as list of tuples, okay. Or process after precompute using index. Simpler:
```
idx=0
N=data[idx]; idx+=1
W=data[idx:idx+N]; idx+=N
L=[0]*N; R=[0]*N
for i in range(N):
    L[i]=data[idx]; R[i]=data[idx+1]; idx+=2
Q=data[idx]; idx+=1
queries=[]
for _ in range(Q):
    s=data[idx]-1; t=data[idx+1]-1; idx+=2
    queries.append((s,t))
```

Build heaps:
```
heapR = [(R[i], i) for i in range(N)]
heapL = [(-L[i], i) for i in range(N)]
heapq.heapify(heapR); heapq.heapify(heapL)
```

BFS:
```
comp = [-1]*N
cid = 0
for start in range(N):
    if comp[start] != -1: continue
    comp[start] = cid
    queue = [start]
    # But if start was already popped from heaps? unvisited means not popped as fresh; but could its heap entries have been popped as stale? no.
    while queue:
        u = queue.pop()  # use list as stack, BFS/DFS both fine
        Lu = L[u]; Ru = R[u]
        # left neighbors
        while heapR and heapR[0][0] < Lu:
            r, v = heapq.heappop(heapR)
            if comp[v] == -1:
                comp[v] = cid
                queue.append(v)
        # right neighbors
        while heapL and -heapL[0][0] > Ru:
            l_neg, v = heapq.heappop(heapL)
            if comp[v] == -1:
                comp[v] = cid
                queue.append(v)
    cid += 1
```
Need note: When we pop from heapR, we might pop a vertex v that is unvisited but has R < Lu. We set comp[v]=cid and append. But v's heapL entry remains. Later, when processing another vertex, heapL may pop v as stale. Good.

However, there is a subtle bug: In BFS, when processing u, we pop all left neighbors from heapR. But suppose among them there is a vertex v that is unvisited, we append. Later in the same processing of u, after finishing left neighbors, we process right neighbors from heapL. Could heapL contain v? Yes. If v also satisfies L_v > R_u (i.e., v is both left of u? impossible: R_v < L_u and L_v > R_u would imply interval v spans across u? Let's check: R_v < L_u <= R_u < L_v, so L_v > R_v, possible? Wait interval v with L_v > R_v? No, L_v <= R_v. Since R_v < L_u <= R_u < L_v, we get R_v < L_v, but interval requires L_v <= R_v, contradiction. So a vertex cannot be both left and right of u. Thus v won't be popped in right neighbors of u. But if v is unvisited and left of u, its L_v <= R_v < L_u <= R_u, so L_v < R_u, so not right. So no duplicate in same vertex. Fine.

But what if a vertex v is left neighbor of u and also right neighbor of some other vertex w processed later; heapL stale skip.

BFS uses stack; no issue.

Now, after BFS, we can free heaps? Not necessary.

Precompute prefR/suffL:
```
MAXC = 2*N
INF = 10**30
bestR = [INF]*(MAXC+2)
bestL = [INF]*(MAXC+2)
for i in range(N):
    if W[i] < bestR[R[i]]: bestR[R[i]] = W[i]
    if W[i] < bestL[L[i]]: bestL[L[i]] = W[i]
prefLE = [INF]*(MAXC+2)
prefLE[0] = bestR[0]  # INF
for c in range(1, MAXC+2):
    prefLE[c] = min(prefLE[c-1], bestR[c])
# prefR(c) = prefLE[c-1] if c>=1 else INF
```

Now, answer queries:
```
out=[]
for s,t in queries:
    if comp[s] != comp[t]:
        out.append("-1")
        continue
    Ls=L[s]; Rs=R[s]; Lt=L[t]; Rt=R[t]
    if Rs < Lt or Rt < Ls:
        out.append(str(W[s]+W[t]))
        continue
    # overlap
    common = min(prefLE[min(Ls,Lt)-1], sufGE[max(Rs,Rt)+1])
    extra = common
    d3a = prefLE[Ls-1] + sufGE[Rt+1]
    d3b = sufGE[Rs+1] + prefLE[Lt-1]
    if d3a < extra: extra = d3a
    if d3b < extra: extra = d3b
    if extra >= INF:
        out.append("-1")
    else:
        out.append(str(W[s]+W[t]+extra))
```

Let's think about edge cases.

### Edge cases

1. N=2, intervals disjoint: BFS comp same. Query direct -> W1+W2.
2. N=2, intervals overlap: G has no edge, comp different. Query -> -1. BFS: start0 process no neighbors, comp0; start1 comp1. Good.
3. Intervals all pairwise overlap: G no edges, each singleton. BFS: each start no neighbors, comp unique. Queries all -1. Distance formulas not used.
4. Intervals with same L/R. Edge if disjoint. BFS handles. prefR/suffL with duplicate coordinates: bestR/bestL min weights. If multiple intervals same coordinate, components? If two identical intervals overlap, no edge between them; they may connect via others. BFS with heaps keyed by R/L: When processing u, if another identical interval v has R_v = R_u, not left (R<L? no); L_v = L_u, not right (L>R? no). So no edge. Good. If an interval x disjoint from both, BFS will discover.

Now, let's revisit the BFS component algorithm with heaps. Is it truly O(N log N)? Each vertex pushed once into heapR and heapL initially. But when we pop from heapR, we might pop a vertex that is unvisited and append; later when processing that vertex, we might pop from heapR again? No, its heapR entry was removed when popped. Its heapL entry remains. Similarly, a vertex visited via heapL has its heapL entry removed, heapR remains. So each vertex popped once from each heap. Total heap pops <= 2N. While loops pop at most 2N times. Heapify O(N). BFS queue O(N). Good.

But wait: In the BFS loop, for each processed u, we do `while heapR and heapR[0][0] < Lu`. Suppose heapR[0] is a stale visited vertex with R < Lu; we pop it. Suppose heapR[0] is an unvisited vertex with R < Lu; we pop and visit. Suppose heapR[0] is a stale visited vertex with R >= Lu; we don't pop. But there might be unvisited vertices with R < Lu behind stale vertices with R >= Lu? Impossible because heap ordered by R; if heapR[0] has R >= Lu, all entries have R >= Lu. So no unvisited with R<Lu remains. Good.

For heapL, heap ordered by -L, so heapR[0] corresponds to largest L. Condition `-heapL[0][0] > Ru` means largest L > Ru. If false, all L <= Ru, so no right neighbors. If true, pop. Good.

Now, one subtlety: We use `comp[v] == -1` to check unvisited. But when we pop a stale visited vertex, we skip. However, what if a vertex is currently in the queue (visited but not processed) and its heap entry is popped from the other heap? It is visited, skip. Fine.

Now, BFS start loop: We iterate `for start in range(N)`. If a vertex was visited, skip. If unvisited, start BFS. But what if an unvisited vertex's heapR entry was popped earlier as stale? Can't happen because stale means visited. If it was popped and unvisited, we would have visited it. So it remains. Good.

Now, let's examine the proof of diameter ≤3 more carefully: We assumed if v1 and v4 comparable, path v0-v1-v4 length2. If v1<v4, edge v1-v4 yes, path v0-v1-v4 uses edges v0-v1 and v1-v4. If v4<v1, edge v1-v4 yes, same. So v0 to v4 length2. But if v1 and v4 comparable, then v0-v1-v4 is a valid path regardless of v0-v4? yes. This contradicts shortest subpath v0-v1-v2-v3-v4 of length4. Thus v1,v4 incomparable. Similarly v0,v3 comparable? If v0<v3 or v3<v0, edge v0-v3, path v0-v3-v4 length2 (since v3-v4 edge). Contradiction. Good.

Then from v1<v0: R1 < L0.
v0 and v3 incomparable => intervals overlap: L0 <= R3 (and L3 <= R0). We only need L0 <= R3.
Thus R1 < L0 <= R3 => R1 < R3.
From v3<v4: R3 < L4.
v1 and v4 incomparable => overlap: L4 <= R1 (and L1 <= R4). We need L4 <= R1.
Thus R3 < L4 <= R1 => R3 < R1.
Contradiction. Perfect.

If first orientation v0 < v1 (v1 right of v0), then by symmetry (mirror intervals: replace L with -R, R with -L) the same proof applies. Or we can reverse the order? Let's see: If v0 < v1, then v1 > v2? Actually orientations alternate: v0 < v1 > v2 < v3 > v4? Wait if v0 < v1 (v1 right of v0), then v1 > v2 (v2 left of v1), v2 < v3, v3 > v4. This is symmetric to previous with "left/right" swapped. The proof with inequalities would also yield contradiction. Good.

Now, one subtle point: In a shortest path, consecutive orientations must alternate. Suppose v0 < v1 and v1 < v2 (both up). Then v0 < v2 by transitivity (R_v0 < L_v1 <= R_v1 < L_v2? Actually v0<v1 means R_v0 < L_v1; v1<v2 means R_v1 < L_v2. Since L_v1 <= R_v1, R_v0 < L_v1 <= R_v1 < L_v2, so R_v0 < L_v2, edge v0-v2). Then path v0-v2-...-vk shorter. So not shortest. Similarly both down. Good.

Let's think about heapL condition and popping all right neighbors. Suppose heapL has stale entries with L > R_u but they are visited; we pop and skip. If heapL top has L <= R_u, no unvisited right neighbor remains because all entries have L <= top? Wait heapL is min-heap by -L, so top has largest L. If largest L <= R_u, then all L <= R_u. So no right neighbor. Good.

Now, let's think about if we should use `queue.pop()` (DFS) vs `pop(0)` (BFS). Both visit components; DFS stack okay. The order of visiting doesn't matter for component discovery. Use list as stack for speed.

Now, let's consider if BFS with heaps might fail to discover a vertex because it was popped from heapR as stale? Stale means visited, so no. If a vertex is unvisited, its heapR entry remains until popped. If it is a left neighbor of some processed vertex, it will be popped and visited. If it is never a left neighbor of any processed vertex but is a right neighbor, it will be popped from heapL. If it is neither left nor right neighbor of any processed vertex, then no edge from processed vertices to it; it will remain unvisited until start loop. Good.

Now, let's think about if a vertex can be discovered via heapR but its heapR entry was already popped earlier as stale? impossible. If it was popped earlier, it would have been visited at that earlier time. So no.

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys
import heapq

INF = 10**30


def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    idx = 0

    N = data[idx]
    idx += 1

    W = data[idx:idx + N]
    idx += N

    L = [0] * N
    R = [0] * N
    for i in range(N):
        L[i] = data[idx]
        R[i] = data[idx + 1]
        idx += 2

    Q = data[idx]
    idx += 1

    queries = []
    for _ in range(Q):
        s = data[idx] - 1
        t = data[idx + 1] - 1
        idx += 2
        queries.append((s, t))

    # Connected components by implicit BFS.
    heapR = [(R[i], i) for i in range(N)]   # by right endpoint
    heapL = [(-L[i], i) for i in range(N)]  # by left endpoint, max-heap
    heapq.heapify(heapR)
    heapq.heapify(heapL)

    comp = [-1] * N
    cid = 0

    for start in range(N):
        if comp[start] != -1:
            continue

        comp[start] = cid
        stack = [start]

        while stack:
            u = stack.pop()
            Lu = L[u]
            Ru = R[u]

            # All unvisited intervals with R < L[u] are neighbors.
            while heapR and heapR[0][0] < Lu:
                _, v = heapq.heappop(heapR)
                if comp[v] == -1:
                    comp[v] = cid
                    stack.append(v)

            # All unvisited intervals with L > R[u] are neighbors.
            while heapL and -heapL[0][0] > Ru:
                _, v = heapq.heappop(heapL)
                if comp[v] == -1:
                    comp[v] = cid
                    stack.append(v)

        cid += 1

    del heapR, heapL

    # Prefix minima of weights by R, suffix minima of weights by L.
    MAXC = 2 * N

    bestR = [INF] * (MAXC + 2)
    bestL = [INF] * (MAXC + 2)

    for i in range(N):
        if W[i] < bestR[R[i]]:
            bestR[R[i]] = W[i]
        if W[i] < bestL[L[i]]:
            bestL[L[i]] = W[i]

    # prefLE[c] = min weight among intervals with R <= c
    prefLE = [INF] * (MAXC + 2)
    prefLE[0] = bestR[0]
    for c in range(1, MAXC + 2):
        prefLE[c] = min(prefLE[c - 1], bestR[c])

    # sufGE[c] = min weight among intervals with L >= c
    sufGE = [INF] * (MAXC + 2)
    sufGE[MAXC + 1] = INF
    for c in range(MAXC, -1, -1):
        sufGE[c] = min(bestL[c], sufGE[c + 1])

    out = []

    for s, t in queries:
        if comp[s] != comp[t]:
            out.append("-1")
            continue

        Ls, Rs = L[s], R[s]
        Lt, Rt = L[t], R[t]

        # Direct edge.
        if Rs < Lt or Rt < Ls:
            out.append(str(W[s] + W[t]))
            continue

        # s and t overlap.
        # Common neighbor: left of both, or right of both.
        common = min(
            prefLE[min(Ls, Lt) - 1],
            sufGE[max(Rs, Rt) + 1]
        )

        extra = common

        # 3-edge paths:
        # s > x < y > t
        d3a = prefLE[Ls - 1] + sufGE[Rt + 1]
        # s < x > y < t
        d3b = sufGE[Rs + 1] + prefLE[Lt - 1]

        if d3a < extra:
            extra = d3a
        if d3b < extra:
            extra = d3b

        if extra >= INF:
            out.append("-1")
        else:
            out.append(str(W[s] + W[t] + extra))

    print("\n".join(out))


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
